Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I was in a car accident with my husband about three weeks ago, and I am here because I can’t remember important parts of it. It really bothers me that there’s just this blank where that night should be.\n\nDoctor: During the car accident, did you hit your head or lose consciousness, even if it was just for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I hit my head or lost consciousness, because everything went hazy right after I saw the other car sliding toward us. There is just a big blank space in my memory between that moment and suddenly being outside on the road with people talking to me. I just can't recall what happened during that gap.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or by a doctor right after the accident, and did they mention if you had a concussion or any head injury?\n\nPatient: I don't recall being taken to the hospital immediately after the crash, and I don't think anyone mentioned a concussion at the scene. However, I did go to a follow-up medical appointment with my doctor later because my daughter insisted on it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing other symptoms like distressing nightmares, sudden flashbacks of the crash, or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have nightmares or flashbacks because of that blank space in my memory, but I am constantly on edge. I feel so tense watching every passing car, and if a door slams or my husband touches my shoulder, I jump out of my skin and my heart races.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble remembering daily things or learning new information, or is your memory loss strictly limited to the time of the crash?\n\nPatient: The actual blank space in my memory is strictly limited to the crash itself, specifically between seeing that car slide and suddenly being outside. However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?"

When her daughter finally insisted on driving her to a follow‑up medical appointment and Misun grew visibly distressed trying to answer questions about the collision, the primary provider recommended a mental health evaluation to better understand how the experience was affecting her day‑to‑day functioning.\n- Chief Complaint: “I can’t remember important parts of the car accident, and it bothers me that there’s just this blank where that night should be.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Ever since the crash, my head feels cloudy, like I’m half-awake, and I lose track of what I’m doing in the middle of simple things like following a recipe or even during Bible study when I can’t follow the passage we’re reading aloud. \n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I keep trying to replay the accident in my mind, but there’s this big empty space between seeing the other car sliding toward us and suddenly being outside on the road with people talking to me, and no matter how hard I think, I just can’t pull those missing pieces back. \n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): My children tell me I should be happy we’re alive and the grandchildren come over to cheer me up, but I just feel flat and far away from it all, like I’m watching someone else’s family visit and I can’t feel the joy I know I’m supposed to feel. \n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Now, even when I’m just sitting in my living room or walking with the seniors, I’m constantly listening for loud noises and watching every car that passes, tense in my shoulders and waiting for something bad to happen again. \n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If a door slams, a car honks outside, or my husband touches my shoulder without warning, I jump out of my skin and my heart races so hard I have to sit down and breathe for several minutes before I feel halfway normal again. \n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): Quite often these past weeks, it feels like I’m moving through a foggy dream, like the house and even my own hands aren’t really mine, and I’ll catch myself thinking, ‘Is this really happening to me?’ as if I’m watching my life from a distance.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped going to my senior-center classes and the walkers club, and I only agree to car rides if my daughter rearranges her schedule to drive me on side streets, which has made it hard to keep my usual appointments and see friends.\n- duration: These strange feelings and reactions have been going on ever since the car accident a little over two weeks ago and haven’t really let up.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I can’t remember important parts of the car accident, and it bothers me that there’s just this blank where that night should be.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. It really bothers me that there’s just this blank where that night should be.\n\nDoctor: During the car accident, did you hit your head or lose consciousness, even if it was just for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I hit my head or lost consciousness, because everything went hazy right after I saw the other car sliding toward us. There is just a big blank space in my memory between that moment and suddenly being outside on the road with people talking to me. I just can't recall what happened during that gap.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or by a doctor right after the accident, and did they mention if you had a concussion or any head injury?\n\nPatient: I don't recall being taken to the hospital immediately after the crash, and I don't think anyone mentioned a concussion at the scene. However, I did go to a follow-up medical appointment with my doctor later because my daughter insisted on it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing other symptoms like distressing nightmares, sudden flashbacks of the crash, or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have nightmares or flashbacks because of that blank space in my memory, but I am constantly on edge. I feel so tense watching every passing car, and if a door slams or my husband touches my shoulder, I jump out of my skin and my heart races.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble remembering daily things or learning new information, or is your memory loss strictly limited to the time of the crash?\n\nPatient: The actual blank space in my memory is strictly limited to the crash itself, specifically between seeing that car slide and suddenly being outside. However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very flat and distant, and when my grandchildren visit, it feels like I'm watching someone else's family because I can't feel the joy I know I'm supposed to feel. Because of how I've been feeling, I have also stopped going to my senior-center classes and my walkers club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced similar gaps in your memory or \"blank spaces\" after experiencing stressful or difficult events earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. This is the very first time I've ever had a blank space in my memory like this.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble with your sleep, such as difficulty falling asleep or waking up frequently during the night?"
  }

However, I did go to a follow-up medical appointment with my doctor later because my daughter insisted on it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing other symptoms like distressing nightmares, sudden flashbacks of the crash, or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have nightmares or flashbacks because of that blank space in my memory, but I am constantly on edge. I feel so tense watching every passing car, and if a door slams or my husband touches my shoulder, I jump out of my skin and my heart races.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble remembering daily things or learning new information, or is your memory loss strictly limited to the time of the crash?\n\nPatient: The actual blank space in my memory is strictly limited to the crash itself, specifically between seeing that car slide and suddenly being outside. However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very flat and distant, and when my grandchildren visit, it feels like I'm watching someone else's family because I can't feel the joy I know I'm supposed to feel. Because of how I've been feeling, I have also stopped going to my senior-center classes and my walkers club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced similar gaps in your memory or \"blank spaces\" after experiencing stressful or difficult events earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. This is the very first time I've ever had a blank space in my memory like this.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble with your sleep, such as difficulty falling asleep or waking up frequently during the night?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major problems with my sleep since the accident, and it has been mostly okay. Compared to all the other stressful things I've been dealing with, my sleep hasn't been much of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing any physical symptoms such as frequent headaches, dizziness, or feeling unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced frequent headaches or physical dizziness since the accident. However, my head has felt very cloudy and foggy ever since that night."

However, I did go to a follow-up medical appointment with my doctor later because my daughter insisted on it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing other symptoms like distressing nightmares, sudden flashbacks of the crash, or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have nightmares or flashbacks because of that blank space in my memory, but I am constantly on edge. I feel so tense watching every passing car, and if a door slams or my husband touches my shoulder, I jump out of my skin and my heart races.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble remembering daily things or learning new information, or is your memory loss strictly limited to the time of the crash?\n\nPatient: The actual blank space in my memory is strictly limited to the crash itself, specifically between seeing that car slide and suddenly being outside. However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very flat and distant, and when my grandchildren visit, it feels like I'm watching someone else's family because I can't feel the joy I know I'm supposed to feel. Because of how I've been feeling, I have also stopped going to my senior-center classes and my walkers club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced similar gaps in your memory or \"blank spaces\" after experiencing stressful or difficult events earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. This is the very first time I've ever had a blank space in my memory like this.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble with your sleep, such as difficulty falling asleep or waking up frequently during the night?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major problems with my sleep since the accident, and it has been mostly okay. Compared to all the other stressful things I've been dealing with, my sleep hasn't been much of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing any physical symptoms such as frequent headaches, dizziness, or feeling unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced frequent headaches or physical dizziness since the accident. However, my head has felt very cloudy and foggy ever since that night.\n\nDoctor: When you hear sudden car noises like brakes screeching or horns honking, do you experience intense distress or physical reactions like shaking or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Yes, if a car honks or there is any sudden noise, I jump right out of my skin. My heart starts racing so hard that I have to sit down and just breathe for several minutes before I feel halfway normal again.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you found yourself carrying strong negative beliefs, such as blaming yourself for what happened, or feeling that the world is completely unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I don't blame myself at all, as it was clearly the other car that swerved and hit us on the ice. But I do feel constantly on edge and tense, always waiting for something bad to happen again even when I'm just sitting in my living room."
  }
]

However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very flat and distant, and when my grandchildren visit, it feels like I'm watching someone else's family because I can't feel the joy I know I'm supposed to feel. Because of how I've been feeling, I have also stopped going to my senior-center classes and my walkers club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced similar gaps in your memory or \"blank spaces\" after experiencing stressful or difficult events earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. This is the very first time I've ever had a blank space in my memory like this.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble with your sleep, such as difficulty falling asleep or waking up frequently during the night?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major problems with my sleep since the accident, and it has been mostly okay. Compared to all the other stressful things I've been dealing with, my sleep hasn't been much of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing any physical symptoms such as frequent headaches, dizziness, or feeling unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced frequent headaches or physical dizziness since the accident. However, my head has felt very cloudy and foggy ever since that night.\n\nDoctor: When you hear sudden car noises like brakes screeching or horns honking, do you experience intense distress or physical reactions like shaking or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Yes, if a car honks or there is any sudden noise, I jump right out of my skin. My heart starts racing so hard that I have to sit down and just breathe for several minutes before I feel halfway normal again.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you found yourself carrying strong negative beliefs, such as blaming yourself for what happened, or feeling that the world is completely unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I don't blame myself at all, as it was clearly the other car that swerved and hit us on the ice. But I do feel constantly on edge and tense, always waiting for something bad to happen again even when I'm just sitting in my living room.\n\nDoctor: Although you don't experience sudden images or flashbacks, do you find yourself having persistent, repetitive thoughts about the accident that you cannot stop or get out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, even though I don't have flashbacks, my mind keeps looping back to that night because I am constantly trying to replay the accident in my head. I keep trying so hard to fill in that blank, empty space of what happened, but no matter how hard I think, I just can't pull those missing pieces back."
  }

I feel so tense watching every passing car, and if a door slams or my husband touches my shoulder, I jump out of my skin and my heart races.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble remembering daily things or learning new information, or is your memory loss strictly limited to the time of the crash?\n\nPatient: The actual blank space in my memory is strictly limited to the crash itself, specifically between seeing that car slide and suddenly being outside. However, in my daily life, my head has been feeling very cloudy and I often lose track of what I’m doing, like when I'm trying to follow a recipe or keep up during Bible study.\n\nDoctor: During that follow-up medical appointment your daughter insisted on, did the doctor perform any brain scans or find any physical evidence of a head injury or concussion?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the doctor performing any brain scans or mentioning a concussion at that appointment. They mostly just asked me questions about the accident, which made me very distressed and led them to recommend this mental health evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been actively trying to avoid thinking or talking about it, or avoiding driving, riding in cars, or going near the crash site?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely avoid the route where the crash happened. I only agree to car rides if my daughter drives me on side streets to bypass that area entirely, which has made it very hard to attend my senior-center classes or see my friends.\n\nDoctor: Even though you have that blank space, do you ever experience sudden, unwanted memories or images of the car sliding toward you popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden, unwanted images or memories of the crash popping into my head. Instead, it is mostly just a frustrating blank space whenever I try to think back to that night.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you felt emotionally numb, distant from your family, or lost interest in the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very flat and distant, and when my grandchildren visit, it feels like I'm watching someone else's family because I can't feel the joy I know I'm supposed to feel. Because of how I've been feeling, I have also stopped going to my senior-center classes and my walkers club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced similar gaps in your memory or \"blank spaces\" after experiencing stressful or difficult events earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. This is the very first time I've ever had a blank space in my memory like this.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had any trouble with your sleep, such as difficulty falling asleep or waking up frequently during the night?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any major problems with my sleep since the accident, and it has been mostly okay. Compared to all the other stressful things I've been dealing with, my sleep hasn't been much of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been experiencing any physical symptoms such as frequent headaches, dizziness, or feeling unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced frequent headaches or physical dizziness since the accident. However, my head has felt very cloudy and foggy ever since that night.\n\nDoctor: When you hear sudden car noises like brakes screeching or horns honking, do you experience intense distress or physical reactions like shaking or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Yes, if a car honks or there is any sudden noise, I jump right out of my skin. My heart starts racing so hard that I have to sit down and just breathe for several minutes before I feel halfway normal again.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you found yourself carrying strong negative beliefs, such as blaming yourself for what happened, or feeling that the world is completely unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I don't blame myself at all, as it was clearly the other car that swerved and hit us on the ice. But I do feel constantly on edge and tense, always waiting for something bad to happen again even when I'm just sitting in my living room.\n\nDoctor: Although you don't experience sudden images or flashbacks, do you find yourself having persistent, repetitive thoughts about the accident that you cannot stop or get out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, even though I don't have flashbacks, my mind keeps looping back to that night because I am constantly trying to replay the accident in my head. I keep trying so hard to fill in that blank, empty space of what happened, but no matter how hard I think, I just can't pull those missing pieces back.\n\nDoctor: To help me understand the timeline better, has it been more than a month since the car accident occurred, or has it been exactly three weeks?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been a full month yet—the accident happened about three weeks ago. All of these strange feelings and reactions started right after the crash and have been going on continuously ever since."
  }

Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.
